Yeah… it’s not only at work. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls all the time, but even at home or if I’m supposed to just sit through something, I get this feeling like pressure building up in my body.

I don’t mean to be difficult or anything, it’s just I’ll kind of pop up or walk off before I’ve really thought about it.\n\nMy supervisor said it’s starting to be a safety issue, so… I thought I should come talk to someone before it gets worse. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about what you're doing before you get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I mean, I don’t want it to sound like I’m just ignoring rules on purpose, because I’m not. My supervisor said it’s becoming a safety problem, so… yeah, I guess that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through in other areas of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll kind of jump into people’s conversations at work, or answer before the lead is even finished asking something.\n\nI usually realize right after, like, “Oh, I should’ve waited,” and then people get irritated with me. I’m not trying to interrupt, it just comes out. I’m not trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or anxious right before you act on impulse or get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think it’s usually like I’m upset first, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I just do it, and then afterward I notice people looking at me or I realize I walked off. I’m not sure if that counts as trouble organizing or if that’s just how I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. At work on the line, even if I know I’m supposed to stay focused on my own station, like one beep from another machine or somebody walking by with a pallet jack can pull my eyes over, and then my whole brain kind of goes with it for a second.\n\nI try not to let it, because I know I’m supposed to be paying attention, but it happens. Is that what you mean by easily distracted?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these impulsive and restless behaviors at home or in your free time, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only at work. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls all the time, but even at home or if I’m supposed to just sit through something, I get this feeling like pressure building up in my body.\n\nLike I need to move, or stretch, or get up and pace, or go do something right then. Same kind of thing happens if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting in training. I try to be respectful and not make it obvious, but it’s hard to just stay still. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you always been like this, even when you were a kid?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything, because I wasn’t trying to be difficult.

I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls all the time, but even at home or if I’m supposed to just sit through something, I get this feeling like pressure building up in my body.\n\nLike I need to move, or stretch, or get up and pace, or go do something right then. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything, because I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut even in elementary school, I remember being the one who couldn’t really stay in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or blurt things out before I was supposed to. I got notes sent home for that kind of stuff… like wandering around the room or speaking out of turn.\n\nI guess I’ve kind of always been like this, just maybe I tried to hide it better as I got older. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors and feelings of restlessness caused problems for you in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to keep it together, and I don’t want to blame everything on that, but it has caused problems.\n\nMostly at work, honestly. I’ve been written up before for safety stuff and production issues, and that scares me because I’m trying to keep the job and work toward getting certified. And… I think some coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much, because they’re not sure I’ll be on top of everything.\n\nI feel bad saying that out loud. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care, because I do care. It’s just… it’s started to affect real things, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these impulsive and restless behaviors consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / production_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even in elementary school, I was the kid who couldn't stay in my seat, talked over the teacher, and got notes sent home for blurting things out or wandering around the room.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s been pretty consistent, honestly… maybe it looks different now because I try harder to control it.

Like, even back in elementary school, I was kind of that kid who had trouble staying in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or blurt things out, or wander around the room, and I’d get notes sent home for it. I don’t think I was trying to be disrespectful or anything… I just had a hard time stopping myself.

So yeah, it’s been there a long time. Maybe the intensity changes depending on the situation, but it’s not really new. Is that what you mean?

Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think there’s been a few months where it just wasn’t there at all.

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about what you're doing before you get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I mean, I don’t want it to sound like I’m just ignoring rules on purpose, because I’m not. It’s more like I’m at my station or in a meeting and then I’m already up or walking off before I’ve really thought it through. My supervisor said it’s becoming a safety problem, so… yeah, I guess that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through in other areas of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll kind of jump into people’s conversations at work, or answer before the lead is even finished asking something.\n\nI usually realize right after, like, “Oh, I should’ve waited,” and then people get irritated with me. I’m not trying to interrupt, it just comes out. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it obvious, but sitting still is hard for me.\n\nLike in certification classes or safety meetings, my foot will be bouncing the whole time, or I’ll be clicking a pen, picking at my sleeves, shifting around in the chair… and sometimes people ask me to stop. I’m not trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or anxious right before you act on impulse or get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think it’s usually like I’m upset first, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I just do it, and then afterward I notice people looking at me or I realize I walked off. Then I get embarrassed or worried I messed up. But right before? I don’t really know that I feel anxious. I haven’t noticed that, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes at work or in other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I try to be careful, especially at work, because I don’t want to mess anything up.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about it more… like, I don’t know if I’d call them careless mistakes. Maybe everybody misses little things sometimes? What kind of details do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble finishing tasks or projects that you start?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… I do try to finish what I start, especially if someone’s counting on me.\n\nSometimes I’ll start something and then it takes longer than I meant, or I kind of need reminders, but I don’t know if that’s “often” or just normal. What would count as having trouble finishing?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or workspace?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I try to keep things kind of together, especially at work. I wouldn’t want anyone thinking I’m messy or not trying.\n\nMy space can get a little all over the place sometimes, but I usually know where things are, I think. With tasks, I kind of just do what’s in front of me first. I’m not sure if that counts as trouble organizing or if that’s just how I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. At work on the line, even if I know I’m supposed to stay focused on my own station, like one beep from another machine or somebody walking by with a pallet jack can pull my eyes over, and then my whole brain kind of goes with it for a second.\n\nI try not to let it, because I know I’m supposed to be paying attention, but it happens. Is that what you mean by easily distracted?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these impulsive and restless behaviors at home or in your free time, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only at work. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls all the time, but even at home or if I’m supposed to just sit through something, I get this feeling like pressure building up in my body.\n\nLike I need to move, or stretch, or get up and pace, or go do something right then. Same kind of thing happens if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting in training. I try to be respectful and not make it obvious, but it’s hard to just stay still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors and feelings of restlessness caused problems for you in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to keep it together, and I don’t want to blame everything on that, but it has caused problems.\n\nMostly at work, honestly. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care, because I do care. It’s just… it’s started to affect real things, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these impulsive and restless behaviors consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent, honestly… maybe it looks different now because I try harder to control it.\n\nLike, even back in elementary school, I was kind of that kid who had trouble staying in my seat. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you didn't experience any of these impulsive or restless behaviors for at least a few months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think there’s been a few months where it just wasn’t there at all.\n\nIt’s been more of a serious problem for maybe the last year and a couple months, especially since I started trying to clean up my habits and work toward forklift and CNC certification. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about what you're doing before you get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I mean, I don’t want it to sound like I’m just ignoring rules on purpose, because I’m not. My supervisor said it’s becoming a safety problem, so… yeah, I guess that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through in other areas of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll kind of jump into people’s conversations at work, or answer before the lead is even finished asking something.\n\nI usually realize right after, like, “Oh, I should’ve waited,” and then people get irritated with me. I’m not trying to interrupt, it just comes out. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it obvious, but sitting still is hard for me.\n\nLike in certification classes or safety meetings, my foot will be bouncing the whole time, or I’ll be clicking a pen, picking at my sleeves, shifting around in the chair… and sometimes people ask me to stop. I’m not trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or anxious right before you act on impulse or get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think it’s usually like I’m upset first, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I just do it, and then afterward I notice people looking at me or I realize I walked off. Then I get embarrassed or worried I messed up. But right before? I don’t really know that I feel anxious. I’m not sure if that counts as trouble organizing or if that’s just how I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I forget normal stuff all the time, like clocking a step on the checklist, answering a supervisor's text, or bringing my safety glasses from my locker, and then it looks like I just don't care.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: If I have to sit for the certification class or a safety meeting, my foot is bouncing, I'm clicking a pen, picking at my sleeves, or shifting around so much people ask me to stop.\n- key: S011\n  name: Leaving_Seat\n  description: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: What brought me in is I keep leaving my station or popping up during meetings when I'm supposed to stay put, and my supervisor says it's becoming a safety problem because I just take off before I even think about it.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. Yeah… I do, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make excuses for it, but I forget normal stuff all the time.

Is that the kind of forgetting you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I’m here because… I’ve been having trouble staying where I’m supposed to be, like at my work station or in meetings. I don’t mean to be difficult or anything, it’s just I’ll kind of pop up or walk off before I’ve really thought about it.\n\nMy supervisor said it’s starting to be a safety issue, so… I thought I should come talk to someone before it gets worse. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about what you're doing before you get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I mean, I don’t want it to sound like I’m just ignoring rules on purpose, because I’m not. It’s more like I’m at my station or in a meeting and then I’m already up or walking off before I’ve really thought it through. My supervisor said it’s becoming a safety problem, so… yeah, I guess that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through in other areas of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll kind of jump into people’s conversations at work, or answer before the lead is even finished asking something.\n\nI usually realize right after, like, “Oh, I should’ve waited,” and then people get irritated with me. I’m not trying to interrupt, it just comes out. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it obvious, but sitting still is hard for me.\n\nLike in certification classes or safety meetings, my foot will be bouncing the whole time, or I’ll be clicking a pen, picking at my sleeves, shifting around in the chair… and sometimes people ask me to stop. I’m not trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or anxious right before you act on impulse or get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think it’s usually like I’m upset first, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I just do it, and then afterward I notice people looking at me or I realize I walked off. Then I get embarrassed or worried I messed up. But right before? I don’t really know that I feel anxious. I haven’t noticed that, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes at work or in other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I try to be careful, especially at work, because I don’t want to mess anything up.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about it more… like, I don’t know if I’d call them careless mistakes. Maybe everybody misses little things sometimes? What kind of details do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble finishing tasks or projects that you start?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… I do try to finish what I start, especially if someone’s counting on me.\n\nSometimes I’ll start something and then it takes longer than I meant, or I kind of need reminders, but I don’t know if that’s “often” or just normal. What would count as having trouble finishing?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or workspace?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I try to keep things kind of together, especially at work. I wouldn’t want anyone thinking I’m messy or not trying.\n\nMy space can get a little all over the place sometimes, but I usually know where things are, I think. With tasks, I kind of just do what’s in front of me first. I’m not sure if that counts as trouble organizing or if that’s just how I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. At work on the line, even if I know I’m supposed to stay focused on my own station, like one beep from another machine or somebody walking by with a pallet jack can pull my eyes over, and then my whole brain kind of goes with it for a second.\n\nI try not to let it, because I know I’m supposed to be paying attention, but it happens. Is that what you mean by easily distracted?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these impulsive and restless behaviors at home or in your free time, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only at work. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls all the time, but even at home or if I’m supposed to just sit through something, I get this feeling like pressure building up in my body.\n\nLike I need to move, or stretch, or get up and pace, or go do something right then. Same kind of thing happens if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting in training. I try to be respectful and not make it obvious, but it’s hard to just stay still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything, because I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut even in elementary school, I remember being the one who couldn’t really stay in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or blurt things out before I was supposed to. I got notes sent home for that kind of stuff… like wandering around the room or speaking out of turn.\n\nI guess I’ve kind of always been like this, just maybe I tried to hide it better as I got older. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors and feelings of restlessness caused problems for you in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to keep it together, and I don’t want to blame everything on that, but it has caused problems.\n\nMostly at work, honestly. I’ve been written up before for safety stuff and production issues, and that scares me because I’m trying to keep the job and work toward getting certified. And… I think some coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much, because they’re not sure I’ll be on top of everything.\n\nI feel bad saying that out loud. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care, because I do care. It’s just… it’s started to affect real things, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these impulsive and restless behaviors consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent, honestly… maybe it looks different now because I try harder to control it.\n\nLike, even back in elementary school, I was kind of that kid who had trouble staying in my seat. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about what you're doing before you get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. My supervisor said it’s becoming a safety problem, so… yeah, I guess that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through in other areas of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll kind of jump into people’s conversations at work, or answer before the lead is even finished asking something.\n\nI usually realize right after, like, “Oh, I should’ve waited,” and then people get irritated with me. I’m not trying to interrupt, it just comes out. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it obvious, but sitting still is hard for me.\n\nLike in certification classes or safety meetings, my foot will be bouncing the whole time, or I’ll be clicking a pen, picking at my sleeves, shifting around in the chair… and sometimes people ask me to stop. I’m not trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or anxious right before you act on impulse or get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think it’s usually like I’m upset first, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I just do it, and then afterward I notice people looking at me or I realize I walked off. Then I get embarrassed or worried I messed up. But right before? I don’t really know that I feel anxious. I haven’t noticed that, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes at work or in other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I try to be careful, especially at work, because I don’t want to mess anything up.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about it more… like, I don’t know if I’d call them careless mistakes. Maybe everybody misses little things sometimes? What kind of details do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble finishing tasks or projects that you start?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… I do try to finish what I start, especially if someone’s counting on me.\n\nSometimes I’ll start something and then it takes longer than I meant, or I kind of need reminders, but I don’t know if that’s “often” or just normal. What would count as having trouble finishing?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or workspace?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I try to keep things kind of together, especially at work. I wouldn’t want anyone thinking I’m messy or not trying.\n\nMy space can get a little all over the place sometimes, but I usually know where things are, I think. With tasks, I kind of just do what’s in front of me first. I’m not sure if that counts as trouble organizing or if that’s just how I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. At work on the line, even if I know I’m supposed to stay focused on my own station, like one beep from another machine or somebody walking by with a pallet jack can pull my eyes over, and then my whole brain kind of goes with it for a second.\n\nI try not to let it, because I know I’m supposed to be paying attention, but it happens. Is that what you mean by easily distracted?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these impulsive and restless behaviors at home or in your free time, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only at work. I’ve been written up before for safety stuff and production issues, and that scares me because I’m trying to keep the job and work toward getting certified. It’s just… it’s started to affect real things, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these impulsive and restless behaviors consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent, honestly… maybe it looks different now because I try harder to control it.\n\nLike, even back in elementary school, I was kind of that kid who had trouble staying in my seat. Same kind of thing happens if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting in training. I try to be respectful and not make it obvious, but it’s hard to just stay still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything, because I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut even in elementary school, I remember being the one who couldn’t really stay in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or blurt things out before I was supposed to. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors and feelings of restlessness caused problems for you in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to keep it together, and I don’t want to blame everything on that, but it has caused problems.\n\nMostly at work, honestly. My supervisor said it’s becoming a safety problem, so… yeah, I guess that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through in other areas of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll kind of jump into people’s conversations at work, or answer before the lead is even finished asking something.\n\nI usually realize right after, like, “Oh, I should’ve waited,” and then people get irritated with me. I’m not trying to interrupt, it just comes out. I’m not trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens. I mean, I try to be careful, especially at work, because I don’t want to mess anything up.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about it more… like, I don’t know if I’d call them careless mistakes. With tasks, I kind of just do what’s in front of me first. I’m not sure if that counts as trouble organizing or if that’s just how I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. At work on the line, even if I know I’m supposed to stay focused on my own station, like one beep from another machine or somebody walking by with a pallet jack can pull my eyes over, and then my whole brain kind of goes with it for a second.\n\nI try not to let it, because I know I’m supposed to be paying attention, but it happens. Is that what you mean by easily distracted?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these impulsive and restless behaviors at home or in your free time, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only at work. Same kind of thing happens if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting in training. I try to be respectful and not make it obvious, but it’s hard to just stay still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything, because I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut even in elementary school, I remember being the one who couldn’t really stay in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or blurt things out before I was supposed to. I got notes sent home for that kind of stuff… like wandering around the room or speaking out of turn.\n\nI guess I’ve kind of always been like this, just maybe I tried to hide it better as I got older. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors and feelings of restlessness caused problems for you in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to keep it together, and I don’t want to blame everything on that, but it has caused problems.\n\nMostly at work, honestly. I’ve been written up before for safety stuff and production issues, and that scares me because I’m trying to keep the job and work toward getting certified. And… I think some coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much, because they’re not sure I’ll be on top of everything.\n\nI feel bad saying that out loud. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care, because I do care. It’s just… it’s started to affect real things, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these impulsive and restless behaviors consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent, honestly… maybe it looks different now because I try harder to control it.\n\nLike, even back in elementary school, I was kind of that kid who had trouble staying in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or blurt things out, or wander around the room, and I’d get notes sent home for it. I don’t think I was trying to be disrespectful or anything… I just had a hard time stopping myself.\n\nSo yeah, it’s been there a long time. Maybe the intensity changes depending on the situation, but it’s not really new. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you didn't experience any of these impulsive or restless behaviors for at least a few months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think there’s been a few months where it just wasn’t there at all.\n\nIt’s been more of a serious problem for maybe the last year and a couple months, especially since I started trying to clean up my habits and work toward forklift and CNC certification. I wanted to be more dependable, so I noticed it more, I guess… or it mattered more.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve had a long stretch where I was just completely calm or not impulsive. Is that answering it right?\n\nDoctor: Did these impulsive and restless behaviors start before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Even in elementary school, before I was 12, I was that kid who couldn’t really stay in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher or blurt things out, and sometimes I’d wander around the room and get notes sent home.\n\nI don’t think I was trying to be bad or anything… it just kind of happened before I could stop myself. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you're supposed to do, like tasks or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I’d like to admit. Or I’ll leave my safety glasses in my locker and not realize until I need them. It makes me feel bad because it probably looks like I just don’t care, but I really am trying. Is that the kind of forgetting you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with sustaining attention in tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. So I have to keep going back over it. I feel kind of embarrassed saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not trying. I am trying… it just doesn’t stick sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these impulsive and restless behaviors in social situations or when you're alone, not just at work or in formal settings?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I’ve been written up before for safety stuff and production issues, and that scares me because I’m trying to keep the job and work toward getting certified. And… I think some coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much, because they’re not sure I’ll be on top of everything.\n\nI feel bad saying that out loud. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care, because I do care. It’s just… it’s started to affect real things, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these impulsive and restless behaviors consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent, honestly… maybe it looks different now because I try harder to control it.\n\nLike, even back in elementary school, I was kind of that kid who had trouble staying in my seat. Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to blame everything on that, but honestly the bigger issue is probably the getting up and moving before I really think.

Like, what actually brought me in was I keep leaving my station, or I’ll pop up during meetings when I’m supposed to stay put. I don’t mean it in a disrespectful way or anything, but my supervisor said it’s starting to be a safety problem because I kind of just take off before I’ve thought it through.

So… yeah, I guess the restlessness and impulsive stuff has caused more trouble than just losing focus. Is that what you mean?

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about what you're doing before you get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. My supervisor said it’s becoming a safety problem, so… yeah, I guess that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through in other areas of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess sometimes. I don’t mean to be rude or anything, but I’ll kind of jump into people’s conversations at work, or answer before the lead is even finished asking something.\n\nI usually realize right after, like, “Oh, I should’ve waited,” and then people get irritated with me. I’m not trying to interrupt, it just comes out. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it obvious, but sitting still is hard for me.\n\nLike in certification classes or safety meetings, my foot will be bouncing the whole time, or I’ll be clicking a pen, picking at my sleeves, shifting around in the chair… and sometimes people ask me to stop. I’m not trying to be annoying, it just kind of happens. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or anxious right before you act on impulse or get up and walk off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think it’s usually like I’m upset first, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I just do it, and then afterward I notice people looking at me or I realize I walked off. With tasks, I kind of just do what’s in front of me first. I’m not sure if that counts as trouble organizing or if that’s just how I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. At work on the line, even if I know I’m supposed to stay focused on my own station, like one beep from another machine or somebody walking by with a pallet jack can pull my eyes over, and then my whole brain kind of goes with it for a second.\n\nI try not to let it, because I know I’m supposed to be paying attention, but it happens. Is that what you mean by easily distracted?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these impulsive and restless behaviors at home or in your free time, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not only at work. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m bouncing off the walls all the time, but even at home or if I’m supposed to just sit through something, I get this feeling like pressure building up in my body.\n\nLike I need to move, or stretch, or get up and pace, or go do something right then. Same kind of thing happens if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting in training. I try to be respectful and not make it obvious, but it’s hard to just stay still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything, because I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut even in elementary school, I remember being the one who couldn’t really stay in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or blurt things out before I was supposed to. I got notes sent home for that kind of stuff… like wandering around the room or speaking out of turn.\n\nI guess I’ve kind of always been like this, just maybe I tried to hide it better as I got older. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors and feelings of restlessness caused problems for you in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to keep it together, and I don’t want to blame everything on that, but it has caused problems.\n\nMostly at work, honestly. I’ve been written up before for safety stuff and production issues, and that scares me because I’m trying to keep the job and work toward getting certified. And… I think some coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much, because they’re not sure I’ll be on top of everything.\n\nI feel bad saying that out loud. I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care, because I do care. It’s just… it’s started to affect real things, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these impulsive and restless behaviors consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent, honestly… maybe it looks different now because I try harder to control it.\n\nLike, even back in elementary school, I was kind of that kid who had trouble staying in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher sometimes, or blurt things out, or wander around the room, and I’d get notes sent home for it. I don’t think I was trying to be disrespectful or anything… I just had a hard time stopping myself.\n\nSo yeah, it’s been there a long time. Maybe the intensity changes depending on the situation, but it’s not really new. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you didn't experience any of these impulsive or restless behaviors for at least a few months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t think there’s been a few months where it just wasn’t there at all.\n\nIt’s been more of a serious problem for maybe the last year and a couple months, especially since I started trying to clean up my habits and work toward forklift and CNC certification. I wanted to be more dependable, so I noticed it more, I guess… or it mattered more.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve had a long stretch where I was just completely calm or not impulsive. Is that answering it right?\n\nDoctor: Did these impulsive and restless behaviors start before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Even in elementary school, before I was 12, I was that kid who couldn’t really stay in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher or blurt things out, and sometimes I’d wander around the room and get notes sent home.\n\nI don’t think I was trying to be bad or anything… it just kind of happened before I could stop myself. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you're supposed to do, like tasks or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of forgetting you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with sustaining attention in tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.
